167. JOHN BLEWITT was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of November, 1 dog cart, value 20 s., the goods of John Riley.
HENRY PEARSON . I am pot-boy at the Lord Duncan Victualling-house. The prisoner came to me and said he had bought a dog-cart for 6 s., and asked me to let him put it in mother's garden, which is in Miles-street, Deptford—I said I would ask mother if he might, and between ten and eleven o'clock that night, I found it in our yard—I saw the prisoner that evening—he came to me and said he had taken the cart to my house—he went away, and next morning it was found in our yard—it is in Court now.
JOHN RILEY . The prisoner called at my house, and asked if I would dispose of a dog-cart; and six or seven weeks afterwards, I missed the dogcart from my yard—this is the cart—I found it before the Magistrate on the Tuesday morning following—I missed it on Wednesday.
LUKE FORD ( police-constable R 75.) I searched for the cart—I saw the prisoner, and asked if he knew any thing of it—he denied it for a long time—Pearson at last came up and said the prisoner had brought the cart to his house—that he at first asked him to let him bring it there, and afterwards told him he had put it in his father's garden—I took him to the yard, where I found the cart in a small garden—I took him to the station-house—he said nothing when I found it—he did not hear what Pearson said—I asked the prisoner if he knew any thing of the cart—he said he did not—I gave my evidence before the Magistrate, and signed it afterwards—I have stated all that passed—the prisoner afterwards told me he had stolen the cart—it did, not strike me at that moment as being important.
Prisoner's Defence. I only meant to take it to give the children a ride.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Three Months.
Before Lord Chief Justice Denman.
[trialAccount t18341124-168]
168. SAMUEL TRIPP was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of November, at Charlton, Kent, 1 ewe, price 30 s., the property of Arthur Drummond, clerk.
JOHN LONGHURST . I am gardener to the Reverend Arthur Drummond, at Charlton, in Kent. On Saturday, the 1st of November, I had twelve sheep of his under my care—they were kept in a field behind the house—it is enclosed with a hedge, except where there are a few hurdles at one side—I saw them all twelve about four o'clock in the afternoon of Saturday—I did not miss any till the middle of the next day (Sunday)—I then missed one ewe—the policeman afterwards produced a skin to me—I knew it to be the skin of my master's sheep—( looking at it)this is it—it is all full of maggots now—I examined it the day it was found (on the Sunday)—it is marked "A. D.," which is the way master's sheep are all marked—I saw the carcass of a sheep at the office at Greenwich, and saw it compared with the skin, and the feet also—I saw the legs of the carcass compared with the feet, and they fitted in exactly—one shoulder and one leg fitted in with the feet—they had formed part of the same sheep—I am quite sure it is the skin of one of his sheep—the shoulder and leg which remained of the carcass fitted the skin—the same policeman has it—it is not fit to produce now—I was quite confident it was the skin of master's sheep—I could swear to it—we had the sheep since the 15th of August—I was familiar with them—they were branded by us, and there was a slight mark of Sir Thomas Wilson's still on the skin—master bought them of him.
THOMAS CANNON . I am a policeman. On Sunday, the 2nd of November, I was on duty at Blackheath, and saw the prisoner in the Dover-road, between two and three o'clock in the morning, coming towards London, in a direction from Charlton, about three-quarters of a mile from where the sheep was killed—I know Mr. Drummond's house—it was about three-quarters of a mile from there—he bad two bundles slung over his shoulder, one in front and one behind—I asked him where he came from that morning—he said he came from Dartford—I asked what he had got in the bundles—he said clothes—I felt the bundle on his back—it felt different to clothes—it was of a harder substance—I said I wished to see what it contained—he made no answer, and I took the bundle off his shoulder—I found it contained the carcass of mutton, warm—it appeared to be recently killed—it was the two hind-quarters—I afterwards examined the other bundles—they contained the two fore-quarters—they appeared warm also, and to be part of the same sheep—I asked Mm how he came by the mutton—he refused to give any account of it—he said nothing when I asked the question, but started, with the intention of making his escape—I secured him, and prevented him—I took him to the station-house with the assistance of another constable—I went to Charlton to make inquiry about the sheep the same morning—I found the skin, head, and feet and entrails, in one of Mr. Drummond's fields—I observed shoemarks in that field, and also in the adjoining field, which was a ploughed field—they led in a direction towards the Dover-road—it was only the foot-marks of one man—I showed the feet of the sheep to Longhurst, and saw them fitted to the meat and skin—the bones were cut away from the joints to compare them, and one in particular has been broken and fitted exactly—in my judgment they formed part of the same legs—I also compared the skin with the quarters of mutton; it appeared to fit.
DANIEL RIORDON . I am a policeman. I was on duty on Blackhealth on Sunday morning—Cannon called me to his assistance—I helped him to take the prisoner—the prisoner seemed to put his hands into his pocket—he put one hand into his right hand trowsers pocket—I took it out, and found a knife in his pocket; it was smeared with blood and grease, and his boots were bloody; and a lump of what seemed, to be mutton fat was on his boots—I saw the bones compared with the feet—they seemed to correspond—about six o'clock that morning I took the prisoner's boot, and compared it with the foot-marks in the field adjoining to where the sheep were kept, and two or three marks in the same field, and leading towards the Dover-road—I fitted the boot to the marks, and found the impression of the nails corresponding with the nails of the boot, and also in the size of it—I counted the nails, and they corresponded.
COURT. Q. Was the ground wet? A. No, it was ploughed ground—I put the boot down on the marks, and saw they corresponded—I did not make a mark with the boot by the side of the old mark—I laid it on the mark I saw—I showed Larking the carcass I found on the prisoner.
THOMAS LARKING . I am a constable of Greenwich, and a butcher by trade. The carcass of a sheep was produced to me by the police-officer, at the magistrate's office, on Monday, the 3rd of November—the sheep was not slaughtered in a butcher-like fashion.
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Transported for Life.
Third Jury, before Mr. Justice Park.
[trialAccount t18341124-169]
169. SAMUEL RICE was indicted for that he, on the 26th of September, at Woolwich, feloniously did forge a certain receipt for money, which is as follows:—"Received, the 26th day of September, 1834, of Messs. Cox and Company, paymasters, Royal Regiment of Artillery, the sum of 20 l., on account of subsistence for my detachment, for the present mont. 20 l. " Henry Pester, Captain." with intents to defraud Richard Henry Cox and another, against the Statute.—2nd and 3rd COUNTS. For feloniously uttering and disposing of a like orged receipt, well knowing it to be forged, with a like intent. 3 other Counts, stating his intent to be to defraud Arthur Barber; 3 other Counts, with intent to defraud Henry Pester; and 3 other Counts, omitting set out the forged instrument.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
ARTHUR BARBER . I am a grocer, and live in Artillery-place, Woolwich. On the 26th of September, the prisoner came to my house and brought this receipt with him—he told me he wanted change for it, for Quarer-master Serjeant Hunter—I gave him 20 l. for it, and requested him to put his name on the back of it, which he did.
Prisoner. Q. Do you recollection what day and what time I brought this receipt to your house? A. On, the 26th of September—I cannot state the hour—I gave the prisoner the cash for it—I cannot say whether I was in the s✗p when he came in—there was a person, named Barker, there—he is no there—I gave the prisoner the cash for the cheque, and I put say name under his name—it might be my son who gave him the pen to put his name on it—I cannot say whether I gave him notes or money—I am sure I gave him 2 l.
WILLIAM HUNTER . I was acting as quarter-master to the detachment
of the Royal artillery, stationed at Woolwich in September last. Cox and Greenwood are agents to the Royal Artillery—when money is wanted for the subsistence of the troops, it is usual to write receipts similar to this, and have them signed by the commander of the detachment—the prisoner was then employed in the office, as second clerk to Captain Pester, who was adjutant; he might have seen these receipts filled up from time to time—it was never any part of his duty to assist in filling them up—when they are filled up, and signed by the commanding officer, we take them either to tradesmen, or to the Woolwich bank, to get cash for them—there is an account of the number and date of the receipts, sent once a year from Cox and Greenwood's, but we give an abstract of the amounts required once a month—we send them an account of the receipts which have been drawn and signed, during the month—that return is made between the 1st and 6th of every month—a receipt, dated on the 26th of September, would be mentioned in the abstract sent between the 1st and 6th of October—the prisoner's employment about the regiment, would give him an opportunity of knowing the abstract would be sent between the 1st and 6th of October—he deserted on the 29th of September—to the best of my belief, the writing in the body of this receipt is the prisoner's—I did not send him to Mr. Barber's on the 26th of September, to get cash for that receipt—I had no knowledge of his going to get cash for it, till after the forgery was disovered.
Prisoner. Q. How can you swear that is my handwriting? A. I swear to the best of my belief—I have seen his writing, similar to that, in the books of the office—I have seen him write that sort of writing—I may have sent him with receipts to get cashed, but I have no recollection—the receipt book has been left on my desk, after I filled a receipt up, and he might have seen it before they were submitted to Captain Pester for signature—I have left my book on the table for five or ten minutes together, and gone out of the office—I cannot say that I never went out of doors, and left the book there—I have no recollection of ever having sent the prisoner for cash for a receipt, but I admit I possibly may have sent him.
CAPTAIN HENRY PESTER . In September last, I commanded a etachment of the Royal Artillery, at Woolwich—the signature of this receipt is not my handwriting—it resembles it very little—I could detect it in an instant—it was not signed by me, or by my authority—I believe he abstract of the receipts was sent to Cox and Greenwood's on the 6th of September—that is the usual day—on forwarding that abstract, it was discovered a receipt of 20 l. was paid, which was not included in it.
WILLIAM ARNOLD . I am a cashier at Cox and Greenwood's, Mr. Cox's Christian names are Richard Henry—there are three partners—I do not know whether there are three members of the firm concerned in the agency of the Royal Artillery—this receipt was presented for payment, and paid by me.
RICHARD RATCLIFFE . I am engaged in the office of the solicites who are employed for Cox and Greenwood—Richard Henry Cox and charles Hammersly are the only two partners concerned in the agency for the Royal Artillery—( receipt read.)
(Captain Pester and William Hunt deposed to the prisoner's previous good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18341124-170]
170. HUGH ALLAN CLARKE and SAMUEL BUSH were indicted for feloniously assaulting Edward Delves, on the 17th of November, at St. Paul's, Deptford, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 4 l.; 8 sovereigns; 3 half-crowns; 3 shillings; and 1 sixpence; his goods and money.
EDWARD DELVES . I live in Mount-Zion, Tunbridge Wells, in Kent. On the 17th of November, between seven and eight o'clock at night, I was in the Lower-road, Rotherhithe—I was intoxicated at the time—I had been drinking the whole day, and was going down to Woolwich—I found myself in the prisoners' company—I have a recollection of that, but how I got into their company I do not know—I went into some public-houses with them—I have a strong knowledge of Bush—the last house we were in was Dawson's, Lower-road, Deptford—I had a pint of beer, and came out—Bush had hold of my arm—Clarke was on before me, and he said to me, "Come along, do not stop so long behind"—Bush said, "Now, you b—, I will do for you," and that is all I can recollect; but I believe Clarke is an innocent man, because he wished to have nothing to do with it—I did not hear him say so—Bush knocked me down—I was in a state of insensibility—I do not know that Clarke did not come up—I received a blow on my forehead, and in my ribs, and on my face—I was sober enough to know that it was Bush knocked me down—I had hold of his arm—before I went to the first public-house with them, I had some silver, but to what amount I do not know—I had no gold.
Q. Have you not sworn you had many sovereigns in your purse? A. Being intoxicated, I was not in a state to declare any thing—I had only silver—if I ever said I had eighteen or nineteen sovereigns, it is not true—I had not a silver watch—I do not know what I had, that is the truth—this "E. Delves," to this deposition, is my handwriting—I do not recollect whether it was read over to me before I signed it—I went before the magistrate on Tuesday; this happened on Monday evening—I had quite lost my blood, and was not in a fit state to be examined.
GEORGE WILSON ( police-constable R 179.) On the night of the 17th of November, I was on duty in Lower-road, Deptford, and in consequence of information, I remained at the bottom of Grove-lane, Lower-road—I saw the prisoners coming up Grove-lane, arm in arm—I went and stopped them, and told them I believed they were the two men I wanted, as a highway robbery had been committed by two marines, and I should stop them on suspicion—Clarke said, "You have got the right two"—I took them into custody—my fellow-constable came up and took Clarke—I took Bush—I searched him, and found in his watch fob 11 s. in silver, and 3 d. in copper.
Cross-examined. Q. You say you were at the bottom of Grove-lane? A. At the top. I saw the men coming up, and spoke to them—neither of them attempted to escape—I did not lay hands on them till I spoke to them—I said, "A robbery has been committed on Plough-road by two marines, and I believe you are the two men that are wanted"—Clarke instantly said, "You have got the right two"—I was alone, but two or three persons were within call, but not in sight—they made no attempt to run off.
ROBERT CLARKE ( police-constable R 167.) I was on duty in Russell-street, Rotherhithe, in the evening, and about ten minutes to ten o'clock I saw both the prisoners coming along out of York-street, going towards Deptford Barracks—Bush asked me the way to the barracks—I directed him the way—he went a few paces, then came back, and asked me if I would
have any thing to drink—I said, "No"—he afterwards came back again, and was jinking money in his pocket—he said, "You had better come and have something to drink, you are a good sort of a chap, both of you; will you come?"—I had another policeman with me—he afterwards offered me a shilling—I said I never drank with persons who were drink—they both appeared drunk—Bush said, "Here is a shilling, you can drink tomorrow"—I said, "You appear flush of money"—he said, "We have got a few friends"—I said, "I recommend you to go to your barracks, for it is late"—a female came to me in about ten minutes, and gave me information, and I went in pursuit of the prisoners towards the barracks—I came to the top of Grove-lane, and saw them turn the corner the moment I turned the corner—my brother constable Wilson came abreast of the two prisoners, and detained them; what passed I cannot say—I received Clarke into my custody, and as I brought him towards the station-house, he looked over his shoulder, and said, "Your brother constable has got the man who has done the deed"—I afterwards asked Clarke if he had any knowledge of passing me in Russell-street—he said he knew what he was taken for, for that they had been drinking—he said he was aware what we took him for, and stated that Bush proposed that he should knock the man down, and Clarke should rob him, and Clarke said he would have nothing to do with it at all; and then they went along towards the barracks, in company with Delves—that Clarke got a-head some hundred yards, then Bush went back, and afterwards came running to him, and said, "I have done him, I have got his silver, but he has no watch," and offered to share the money; but he (Clarke) said, "No, I will have nothing to do with the money at all; you will get yourself transported, and me likewise"—that, as they came along, Bush offered him half-a-crown to get something to drink, but he said, "No, I will have none of the money; I wish I had not come out with you"—when I took Clarke into custody, I heard some coppers fall, I do not know from whom—I searched him at the station-house, but found nothing on him.
Cross-examined. Q. At what hour did you see them again in Grove-Iane? A. I suppose a quarter or ten minutes after ten o'clock, or it might be half an hour—when I came up my brother policeman had stopped them—I had about a hundred yards to come—I could see him stop them—Clarke was already in custody of the other policeman, when he told me he was aware what I took him for—I was not near enough to hear what passed between them and my brother policeman—I had not seen Wilson from the time I had seen them at Rotherhithe till I saw him take them—Clarke told this story openly the moment he was accused.
(The prosecutor's deposition was here read, in which he stated that he had been robbed of a purse containing about eighteen or nineteen sovereigns, some loose silver and copper, and a silver watch, for which he had given 4 l. 10 s.; and that his father had given him the money to purchase it the day before.)
GEORGE WILSON re-examined. I stopped them in Grove-lane—I had received information of the robbery perhaps half an hour before, or not so long—my beat joins Rotherhithe—York-street is not five minutes' walk from Grove-lane—my brother constable, Clarke, gave me the information—I was present when Bush was searched—I did not see Clarke searched—my attention was directed to Bush.
GEORGE DAWSON . I keep a public-house in Lower-road, Rotherhithe. The prisoners came there on the 17th, with the prosecutor in their company
—I heard nothing pass—they had a pot of porter, and asked for more—they all three left the house together—about twenty minutes, or half an hour afterwards, I saw the prosecutor—he was bleeding, and his face was swollen.
SAMUEL DELVES re-examined. Q. You were in many public-houses with these men drinking? A. So I believe—I do not know how long I was in their company—the name of the last house is the Red Lion—on coming out of the Red Lion I had hold of Bush's arm—Clarke was on before—he sung out, with some seafaring phrases, "Come on, why do you stay so long?"—Bush gave me a blow, and said, "I have done for you"—I am certain the man who sung out was Clarke.
COURT. Q. Is it true or false that your father gave you 4 l. 10 s. the day before (the 16th of November,) to buy a watch with? A. He did not give it me to buy a watch—he has given me money, 5 l. 10 s.—he did not on the 16th of November—he gave me money to buy a watch a good bit ago, more than a year ago—I had no sovereigns about me, to my knowledge—I do not know—I do not think I had—I had some silver—I was not earning more than 10 s. a week at the time—I did not give 4 l. 10 s. for a watch the day before—I was hardly in a state to give evidence when I was before the Magistrate, I was so bloody—I have not lived at Mount Zion, Tunbridge-Wells, for some time—I should think it is nearly two years—I now live with my father—I have been to sea since that—I was going to Woolwich, to go on board a government steam-boat.
NOT GUILTY .
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18341124-171]
171. JOHN DIXON, WILLIAM INGRAM , and JAMES NEALE were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 2 shirts, value 12 s., the goods of William McIntosh.
SUSANNAH MCINTOSH . I am the wife of William McIntosh, who is a corporal of the sappers and miners. I am a laundress, and live in Rope-yard-walk, Woolwich—I hung two shirts and some other things out to dry, on the 7th of November, in the pound which my husband had charge of—I missed the two shirts about six o'clock in the evening—one of these shirts is the property of Mr. Wilton.
SAMUEL NICHOLS . I received information, and took these three prisoners in High-street, Woolwich, about nine o'clock that night—I did not find the shirts on them.
GEORGE HAWKINS . I live in Waterman's-fields, Woolwich—I was at the Royal Oak that night—the prisoner Dixon came in with three or four more persons—he had two shirts, and he offered them for sale—he showed me the linen shirt, and I bought it for 2 s.—he said it was his own, and that he had several more for sale—Dixon and the others were in fatigue dresses, as they are now.
EDWARD RUTLAND . I am a gardener, and live at Woolwich. I was at the Royal Oak that night, and saw the three prisoners come in together—Ingram asked if there was any one wanted to buy two shirts, which he would sell for 2 s.—I looked at one, which was the cotton shirt—I told him it was a very small shirt, not fit for a man—it had a seam down the front, and one behind—he said he had a better shirt than that, and he went out as he said to pull it off—I do not know whether he did or not—the brought it in—I looked at it, and saw on the tail of it "H. H."—I said I would not mind giving 1 s. 4 d. for it, which be said he would take, and I
went home for the money, and when I came back he said he had sold it—I said I was very glad, for I did not think it was honestly come by—this is the cotton shirt that he offered first—it was damp—this is the other—this was dry.
HENRY WILTON . This linen shirt is mine—it cost 2 s. 6 d. a yard—I do not know what it is worth—I suppose 9 s. or 10 s.—I have known Dixon some years—he is a most upright man—the last man in the corps that would be guilty of such an action.
(The prisoners received a good character.)
DIXON.— GUILTY . Aged 28.
INGRAM.— GUILTY . Aged 26.
Confined for Fourteen Days. the last seven to be solitary.
NEALE.— NOT GUILTY .
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

172. JAMES MOULTON was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM GATTEY . I am a butcher, and live at Lewisham. The prisoner was in my employ for more than a year and a half—he was in the habit of receiving money for me—Mr. Matthews was a customer of mine—when the prisoner received money, he was to pay it me.
THOMAS MATTHEWS . I am a publican. I dealt with the prosecutor—I paid the prisoner several sums of money in October, when he brought meat—the last bill I have was about the 25th of September, which was 2 s. 10 d., but it has no date to it—I have some former accounts, but none later than this—I think I paid him in October, and in November, but have no papers.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18341124-173]
173. JAMES MOULTON was again indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM GATTEY . The prisoner was my servant, and was in the habit of delivering meat to Mr. Matthews without any note—he told me it was not paid for, and I entered it in my book, but it was not signed by the prisoner—here is the copy of my book—"October the 22nd, Pork, 10 d.—October the 25th, beef steak, and veal, 2 s. 2 d."—the whole bill amounts to 19 s. 10 1/2 d.—the prisoner has not accounted to me for any of this—I asked him whether it had been paid—he said "No."
Prisoner. I never received the money—there is no proof that it has been paid.
THOMAS MATTHEWS . I can swear I paid the prisoner these sums as the meat was delivered, but I have no receipt since the 25th of September.
NOT GUILTY .

174. MARY WELSH was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH LAW, JUN . I live at Greenwich. The prisoner came to my mother's for 1lb. of flour—it came to 2 1/2 d.—she gave me a 6 d.—I gave her change, and put the 6 d. in the till on a shelf—there was another 6 d. there—in half an hour my mother saw the two sixpences in the till—the prisoner came again the next morning, and asked for a 2 d. loaf—she gave another 6 d.—I gave her change, and put that 6 d. in the till—there was no other 6 d. there then—my mother took that 6 d. away in about half an hour—on Friday morning, which was two or three days afterwards, the prisoner came again, and asked for two penny loaves—she gave me a 6 d.—I thought it was bad, and called my father—he came, and told her it was a bad one—she took it back and gave a good one—she then went away.
ELIZABETH LAW . I am mother of this little girl. My husband's name is Joseph Law—on Monday, the 27th of October, between ten and eleven o'clock, I went to the till—there were two sixpences in it, and one of them was bad—I asked my daughter where she took it—she said of an old woman, for 1lb. of flour—I put the bad 6 d. by itself, and have since given it to the officer—the next day I went to the till again, and found only one 6 d. in it, which was bad—I took that, and put it with the other bad one—I gave them both to the officer.
JAMES WILD ( police-constable R 141.) I produce the two sixpences which I received from Mrs. Law.
HARRIET HARDY . I live at No. 18, York-street, Greenwich, with my brother, who keeps a shop. On Saturday, the 1st of November, the prisoner came in for a loaf—she gave me 6 d.—I gave her change, and put into a bowl in the till—there was no other 6 d. in the bowl—Elizabeth Griffiths came in in about ten minutes, and said something—I looked at the 6 d. and found it was bad—the officer came in with the prisoner soon afterwards—I marked the 6 d., and gave it to the officer.
JOHN EMANUEL DURRANT . I am nephew to Mrs. Griffiths, of Greenwich. The prisoner came to the house that day for a pint of beer—she gave me a 6 d.—I gave it to my aunt, who said it was bad, and gave it back to the prisoner, who then gave a good 6 d., and my aunt gave her change.
ELIZABETH GRIFFITHS . I am aunt of this witness. The prisoner offered the bad 6 d., and I refused it—she said she had been taking change at the baker's shop, at the back—there is a baker's there—I then went to Miss Hardy, and gave her information, and the prisoner was taken: and as she had been near the cinder-box, in my tap-room, when there was a talk of having her searched, it was examined when she was gone, and a bad 6 d. was found in it.
Prisoner. If I had had it, I could have thrown it into the fire when I warmed my beer.
JOHN JULIAN ( police-serjeant R 17.) I took the prisoner at the Bee Hive public-house—this is the 6 d. I received of Hardy.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin—I have examined all the sixpences—they are all bad, and from the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I sell pin-cushions, stay-laces, and children's books—I only knew one 6 d. was bad, and I thought it very hard I should be a loser—I offered it, but I do not know where I took it.
GUILTY . Aged 58.— Confined Eighteen Months.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18341124-175]
175. ASHER WICKHAM was indicted for that he, on the 17th of October, feloniously did forge a request for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud James Dodd.—2nd COUNT. For uttering the same, with a like intent.
JOHN CAMPION . I am an officer of the Court of Requests. I know the prisoner, and used to employ him occasionally—I dealt with Mr. James Dodd, for shoes.
JAMES DODD . I am a shoemaker, and live at Woolwich. I supply Mr. Campion with shoes. On the 17th of October, the prisoner came and brought this order, and I let him have the shoes, ( read)—"Oct. 17, 1834. Sir, You may let the bearer have a pair of shoes, and I will see he pays you.
Yours, J. Campion."
MR. CAMPION. This is not my writing, nor did I send it.
THOMAS PACEY BIRT . I am a pawnbroker, and live at Woolwich. I took these shoes in pledge from the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I pawned a pair of shoes at his shop, but I know nothing about the order—I was never in Mr. Dodd's shop.
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Transported for Seven Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoner.
[trialAccount t18341124-176]
176. WILLIAM HEATH was indicted for assaulting Mary Ann Hassel, on the 21st of October, at Charlton, near Greenwich.
MARY ANN HASSELL . I am a single woman, and live with my parents in Bell-street, Greenwich. On the 21st of October, I went with a friend to Charlton fair—I saw the prisoner there—he asked me to go into a show—he is an artillery-man—I went into the show, and, when we came out, he asked me to go and have something to drink—we had some rum and water at a public-house—he was quite a stranger to me, but he said he would see me home, and we walked arm-in-arm till we got to Black heath—he then threw me down—he had kissed me before he threw me down—I did not resist when he kissed me, but I resisted when he threw me down—I called to him to let me get up, but he would not—I called for assistance—he did not take any liberties with me of any kind, but he threw me down, and put his hand on my mouth—he was taken immediately, and has been in custody ever since—Mr. Pershouse came to my assistance—I did not know him before he came up, but he knew me—I had been thrown down against my consent.
Prisoner. Q. Were we not dancing and waltzing together, and humming a tune as people do when they dance without music? A. I was not; but he was singing to himself as he walked along with me—we were not dancing as we came along—it was not by accident that I fell—he took me round the neck, twisted me round, and threw me down—he proposed to go the most private way, but I thought I had a friend in him.
WILLIAM PERSHOUSE . I am a cow-keeper and farmer, and live at Greenwich. I did not know this witness till she recognized me—I know her father—I was coming home in my gig, and saw two persons come across the road, making to this spot—I looked back and saw the prisoner and this witness on the ground—she screamed out, "For God's sake, come to my assistance"—I must have passed just by them, within half a dozen yards, as I had just come out of the main road under the fence, but my horse had shyed at something at the time I saw the persons running to the place: had it not been for that, I could not have passed without seeing the prisoner and the woman—she might have screamed at the time I passed without my hearing her, as my gig made a noise—the moment I stopped I heard her—I went to her, and found her dress very disorderly; her belt was undone, and the back of her clothes very disorderly—the prisoner's dress was not at all undone.
WILLIAM YEOMAN . I am a hair-dresser. I was on the heath with my wife and a friend—when we heard the screams I ran across with my friend, and found the prisoner and the female on the ground.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had any intention of acting in the way that is alleged against me—I considered, by the way she conducted herself, that she was a very imprudent woman.
GUILTY. Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Judgment Respited.

177. WILLIAM NORTON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of October, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Charles Parrott, from his person.
CHARLES PARROTT . I live in Tooley-atreet, in the borough of Southwark. I was returning from the City to my own house, on the night of the 24th of October, with my wife, walking arm in arm—I suddenly felt her shake my arm violently—I immediately looked behind, and saw the prisoner throw my handkerchief behind him—I collared him and the handkerchief was picked up.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you certain yon saw the handkerchief in his hand? A. I am; there were two persons in company with him—he was close to me—nearly close behind me—this happened in the Borough.
ELIZABETH PARROTT . I am the wife of Charles Parrott. I was walking with my husband about half-past five o'clock on the afternoon of the 24th of October—I felt somebody touch the sleeve of ray dress—I instantly looked round, and saw the prisoner draw the handkerchief from my husband's pocket—I shook Mr. Parrott violently, who turned round, and the prisoner dropped the handkerchief from his hand—my husband collared him—I took it up myself, and gave it to the policeman.
( Property produced, and sworn to.)
(Thomas Watts, publican, Hoxton-square; William Saunders, High-street, Shadwell; Charles Sidwell, Gibraltar-gardens, Bethnal-green; and Elizabeth Folson, of Peter-street, deposed to the prisoner's good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined for Six Months.
[trialAccount t18341124-178]
178. THOMAS RYMER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 1 music-stand, value £1, the goods of George Miller.
GEORGE MILLER . I live at No. 18, Bridgehouse-place, Newington-causeway, in the parish of St. George the Martyr, Southwark. I had a music-stand on my premises—on the 7th of November, just at dusk, a lady came and said a man had ran away with a music-stand—I looked across the road and saw a man running with it—I ran after him; and when I got within a few yards of him, he threw it down—a policeman took the prisoner in the next street—this is my stand.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it outside your door? A. Yes, on a sofa—it was dusk—there were a great many persons in the street—I cannot swear it was the prisoner who was carrying it.
ROBERT BROOKS . I live at Newington. I was going along between
seven and eight o'clock that evening—the prisoner ran by me with a music-stand—he struck me on the shoulder with it—that was about forty yards from the prosecutor's, on the opposite side—he was going on very fast, and the stand struck two ladies—the prisoner then stopped for a minute to disengage the stand from them; and there being a glare of light from Mr. Steven's shop, I had a full opportunity of seeing his features—I saw him run down the next street, and soon afterwards I saw him in custody of the officer—I recognised him as being the person who ran against me.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not mean he gave you a blow designedly? A. No; he was going in a hurried manner, and touched me with it—there were a good many persons in the street, and some between him and me—when I saw his face it was quite dark—it could not be three minutes that I had an opportunity of seeing him—I think he had a black cravat on—he had a high-crowned hat on, and a blue body-coat—I was about six yards from him.
FREDERICK BIGGS ( policeman M 92.) I stopped the prisoner in Portland-place—I had heard a cry of "Stop thief," for about three minutes before—I saw Mr. Brooks—he said, in the prisoner's presence, that he was the man—the prisoner made no reply then, but as I was bringing him back, he said he had it gave to him—I asked him what he had gave him?—he said he would not tell me.
(Mr. Graham, tailor, Lillypot-lane, and Mary Powell, deposed to the prisoner's good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined for Six Months.
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18341124-180]
180. MARY ANN ALLUM was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of November, 3 pints of rum, value 5 s.; and 2 glass bottles, value 4 d.; the goods of Joseph Francis Bessey, her master.
ELIZABETH BESSEY . I am the wife of Joseph Francis Bessey, and live in the Wands worth-road. The prisoner came into my service on the 10th of November, and had only been five days with us—she was our only servant—on the 15th, about one o'clock, I went to the wine cellar—we are not in business—the wine cellar is an inner cellar—the outer cellar is the beer cellar—she was in the kitchen—I took out a bottle of port wine, and locked the inner cellar door—I went up stairs, and put the key in its place—she was then in the kitchen—about half-past two o'clock I walked up the garden, and saw the key of the outer cellar taken from outside the lock, where it always was, and found she had locked herself in the outer cellar—she had no business there at all—I knocked at the door—she made no answer—I went round the garden, and returned—I saw the outer door open, but the key was not in the lock—I asked her for the key—she hesitated to give it to me—I pushed the door open, and saw the wine cellar door ajar—it had been forced—I saw a chisel which had been taken out of the tool box, and laid on a box—I came out and told her that was the tool, I supposed, she had done it with—the door had the appearance of a chisel having been used—I told her she should not remain another night in my house—she made no reply—I sent for an officer, and went with him into her own bed-room—I called her up stairs, and examined her boxes—I found nothing there—at last I found two bottles of rum between her
two featherbeds; she was drunk, and smelt of liquor—she said it was not my rum, but she could hardly answer me—I have no doubt of it being ours, for my own handwriting was on the corks of the bottles.
WILLIAM POWELL . I am a policeman. I was sent for to the house—I went into the prisoner's bed-room—on the 15th of November, the prisoner told her mistress the rum was not hers—two bottles of rum were found between the beds; one of them half empty—she smelt of liquor, but was not intoxicated.
ELIZABETH BESSEY . My handwriting is on these corks.
Prisoner's Defence (written.)My mistress came to me in the wood-house, where I was getting some wood, and said she did not think all was right—I said I did not understand her, and begged to know what she meant—she said the cellar door was unlocked, and asked how it came so—to which I replied, that I did not know any thing about it, or that it was unlocked—she said it was very strange, and that, if I would tell the truth, she would not hurt me—some words arose, and I was desired to leave the house that night—she then went out and fetched a policeman—there was not any thing belonging to my mistress found in my boxes—the policeman left the room, but my mistress remained whilst I changed my dress; and as I was stooping to fasten my boots, she asked me what I was picking up—I answered, I was not picking up any thing—the policeman was then called in to search the bed, which consisted of two featherbeds, under which, next to the mattress, he found two bottles of rum, which my mistress said were her property—the reason I did not discover them during the four nights I slept at my mistress's house, is that when I made the bed, I did not remove the uppermost bed from off the bedstead, and never saw them till they were taken from there by the policeman.
ELIZABETH BESSEY . The chisel was ours—there was no mattress on the bed.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Third Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18341124-181]
181. ELIZA JAMES was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of October, 4 half-crowns, 5 shillings, and 4 sixpences, the monies of William Patmor Bolt, from his person; and that she had before been convicted of felony.
WILLIAM PATMOR BOLT . I met the prisoner, on the 19th of October, in Union-street, St. George-the-Martyr—I agreed to go home with her for the night, and gave her 3 s.—we went to bed in Kent-street—I did not know her before—I went to sleep—I had 17 s. when I went there, in my purse in my fob—there were four half-crowns, and the rest shillings' and sixpences—this was between eleven and twelve o'clock at night—I did not sit there and drink—I awoke between twelve and one o'clock, and found she was gone—I missed my money—I came down to the street, and complained to the police—I stopped in the street, and the prisoner came there, accompanied by two others—I took her, and said she had robbed me of the money—she was in the act of returning the money, when the policeman took her, and I gave her in charge—she was undressed when she went to bed, and I expected her to stay there all night.
Prisoner. I never saw him till he came there and accused me of robbing him. Witness. I am certain she is the person—there was a candle in the room—I was perfectly sober—nothing particular passed—I can swear she is the woman—I have not the least doubt of her—she said if I
would not give her in charge of the police, she would return the money—I said I would give her in charge.
RICHARD WHARTLEY ( police-constable M 161.) I was on my beat—the prosecutor came to me—I took the prisoner into custody—the prosecutor was standing with me, when the prisoner and two girls came out of the White Bear-gardens—he took her by the arm, and charged her with robbing him—she put her right hand into her gown, took some thing out, and said to the other girls, "Here, take this"—I crossed the street, took hold of her hand, and kept hold of it till she got to the station-house—I found she had 3 half-crowns and 2 shillings, and I found 2 d. in her pocket.
Prisoner. I told the prosecutor he was mistaken in my person. Witness, She did not tell him so in my hearing—the prosecutor said he had lost 17 s.—he did not say in what coin—he was perfectly sober.
ROBERT IRESON . I am a policeman. I have a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace ( read)—she is the person who was convicted.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18341124-182]
182. WILLIAM AMER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 2 sovereigns, and 1 half-sovereign, the monies of Gervase Stanley, his master.
GERVASE STANLEY . I am an ironmonger, and live in Upper-ground-street, Blackfriars'-road. The prisoner was my porter for three months—on the 25th of October, a customer paid me two sovereigns and a half, which I put into the till—the prisoner, my apprentice, and another person, were there at the time—I then went out, and, when I returned, I looked into the till, and missed the two sovereigns and a half—the prisoner was then gone—he did not return for his wages, which were due that night.
EDWARD ELLIOTT ( police-constable L 111.) I took the prisoner, on the 7th of November, in Blackfriars-road—I did not ask him about the money, but he told me he had taken it; and he said the same at the station-house.
Prisoner's Defence. I gave myself up to him—I was going to Mr. Stanley's—I had heard on the Sunday that the money was missing; and I did not go, supposing I should be suspected of having taken it.
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Transported for Seven Years.
First Jury, before Lord Chief Justice Denman.
[trialAccount t18341124-183]
183. JOSEPH PAINE and JOHN COOPER were indicted for that they, about two o'clock in the night of the 17th of September, at St. Mary, Newington, the dwelling-house of John William Prior feloniously and burglariously did break and enter, with intent the goods therein feloniously and burglariously to steal.
JOHN WILLIAM PRIOR . I am a housekeeper, and Jive at No. 67, Newington-causeway, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington, Surrey. On the 17th of September I went to bed about twelve o'clock at night—I saw the doors fastened, and the windows also—between one and two o'clock in th'e morning I was awoke by a ringing at my bell, and knocking at the door also—I called to know what was the matter—they
told me to come down directly, for my premises were entered by thieves—I went down, opened the street door, and went out—I found a shutter-box, which is in a line with my shop window, was broken open—the lock was broken—the box holds my shutters in the daytime—it was empty that night, but it had been locked up—I looked into the shutter-box, and found a hat; and in that hat a blue bag—I told the policeman, and gave them to him—I do not know which policeman I gave them to—it was one of the two policemen—they had the prisoner Paine down on the pavement at that time—I examined the lock of the shutter-box with the key I lock it up with, and found the key would not act—the lock had been picked, or something—the key would not turn it—one end of the shutter-box communicates with the inside of my shop window which holds the silver goods—a person could get through that end into the window—I am a silversmith—they could get into the shop window from the box, and so into the house—the shop is part of the house—no property was moved at all—the inspector has a plan of the premises.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How do you open the shutter-box from the outside? A. With a key—the shutter-box was made after the house was built—the shutters rest against the wood work, which is the lining of the box, in the daytime, not against any window—the window is on the right hand of the shutter-box—there is a wall at the back of the shutter-box—the left hand side of the box is wood pannelling, on the right hand side is the window—the window is all glass—there is a door which locks with a small key—it is a glass door—that is inside the shop—it was secured by the shutters outside.
Q. When a person has got into the shutter-box, what have they to do to get into the house? A. To cut through this end into the window—they would have to cut through a wood pannelling, on the other side of which is a sheet of iron, which I put up for my own security, which they were not aware of—it was a plate of sheet iron—the shutter-box partly projects from the house like a watch-box.
COURT. Q. There is no possibility of getting from the shutter-box into the house, except by cutting through the pannelling and the sheet of iron? A. No, neither of them were cut—the box locks outside—it is merely used as a shutter-box.
—I am a policeman. The shutter-box is outside, and attached to the house—it cannot possibly be called part of the house.
(The Court ruled that this was not an entry of the dwelling-house.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Bolland.

184. ELIZABETH LEFEVRE and ELIZABETH M'ALLISTER were indicted for that they, on the 5th of September, at St. Mary, Newington, in and upon Francis Lasman, unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously, did make an assault, and unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously did cut and wound him, in and upon his head, with intent feloniously, wilfully, and of malice aforethought, to kill and murder him. Two other Counts, stating their intention to be to disable, or to do him some grievous bodily harm, against the Statute, &c.
The prosecutor being called, did not appear, and his recognizances were estreated.
NOT GUILTY .
