190. ROBERT HENRY FRANKS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES TWIMLOW . I am a bombardier in the Artillery. In June last my detachment was stationed at Woolwich—the prisoner enlisted in the Artillery in June this year—I was present before Mr. Stace, the magistrate, when the prisoner was taken before him to be attested—I saw him sign this paper before Mr. Stace—I know it to be his handwriting—I gave him 2 s.6 d. in the presence of Mr. Stace—I believe that is the usual course—I do not know of his receiving any thing after that, either in money or public stores—I do not know the date in June when he was enlisted.
WILLIAM STACE, ESQ . I am Justice of the Peace for the county of Kent—I have acted as such. Here is my handwriting to this attestation—I remember the prisoner coming before me to make this attestation—I read over distinctly to him myself the questions and answers, and afterwards administered the oath to him—he clearly understood what he was swearing to.
COURT. Q. Is this interlineation yours? A. Yes—I found he had not enlisted according to the Act of Parliament; and by inquiring, I found he had been enlisted on the 12th instead of the 13th—the 13th was inserted before he came to me—I do not know who had written it—I learned from the bombardier, who brought him before me, that he had been enlisted on the 12th—the prisoner was present, and did not contradict that—the whole of the paper was read over to him—when we came to the question, what day it was? it was altered before we got any further—the word "twelve" was inserted before he swore to it—when I came to that question, I stopped and made the alteration.
(This document was here put in and read, in which the prisoner deposed that he was not married—that he had never served either in the army, marines, ordnance, or navy—that he was enlisted in the Royal Artillery on the 12th of June, 1834, by Adjutant Kendall.)
CAPTAIN KENDALL . I am captain of the eighth batalion of the Royal Artillery. The prisoner was brought before me to enlist on the 12th, to the best of my recollection—I do not remember at what time, but I think it was in the morning—I was not before Mr. Stace when he was taken to him to be attested—he continued in the service about two months, when an application was made by a female to see him—I saw her, in his presence, about a fortnight after her first application—I sent for him, to know whether the woman who had made application at the barracks was his wife—I heard from the colonel of an application, made by the woman, for her husband to live out of the barracks—it is customary to allow married men to live out of the barracks—the woman came to ask that permission, and I sent for him, to ask if he was married—he said "Yes"—being married, and having sworn he was not, I said I should not allow him to go out—he made no observation in reply.
Prisoner. Q. Where did you see me on the 12th of June? A. I really cannot call to mind—I do not know that he slept in the Tower of London on the 12th of June—I did not see the prisoner and the woman together at first—I saw them together between two and three months after he was in the regiment—what she said about it was not in the prisoner's presence—I cannot of my own knowledge say whether he was married on the 14th of June, or afterwards—I have not directed any inquiry to be made at St. Mary, Newmarket, where he said he was born.
THOMAS CORBETT . I am a corporal in the 40th company of the Royal Marines. I became acquainted with the prisoner in January, 1831—he embarked with me at Portsmouth, in his Majesty's ship, Ganges, as a private marine—he mustered and did duty in the marines as one of the privates—he belonged to the Portsmouth division—he received allowance as a marine, and continued on board the Ganges with me about fourteen months—he was all that time subject to discipline and doing duty as marine—he wore the uniform—he was never punished, to my knowledge.
WILLIAM LASSON . I belong to the 32nd company of marines. I was serving on board the Ganges, two or three years ago—I saw the prisoner on board that ship, in 1831, and knew him on board for about fourteen months—he was a private in the marines during all that time—after he left the ship, he was flogged at Portsmouth—he was then a private—I do not know whether he remained in the service after he was flogged.
WILLIAM HASKET . I am quarter master Serjeant of the 8th battalion of marines. I saw the prisoner write in the month of July last—I believe the signature "Robert Franks" to this attestation to be his handwriting.
(This attestation was here read. It was one made by the prisoner on the 12th of December, 1829, on his enlisting in the Royal Marines.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was not aware of the questions being in the attestation.
WILLIAM STACE, ESQ . re-examined. I read the questions and answers to him distinctly. He did not take the paper in his hand—I put the questions to him, and read him the answers also—the paper comes to me with the answers written, and he swore to the truth of it.
GUILTY, as to the previous service only. Aged 23.— Recommended to
mercy.— Confined One Month.
Third Jury, before Sir John Cross, Knt.
[trialAccount t18341124-191]
191. HENRY CRONK was indicted for killing and slaying James Chorrington.
HENRY FRANKLIN . On the 5th of September, at a quarter before seven o'clock, I saw a waggon, with four horses, at length proceeding from Black-friars'-bridge—the driver was by the next horse to the shaft-horse, commonly called the body horse—he was on the left hand side of the road, and the horse was at the right-hand side of him—I was about five yards behind the waggon on the carriage road—I saw a string of five horses coming after the waggon—there was a rider on the near side horse of the string, which was the foremost of the five horses—the deceased was about fifteen feet from the curb, and about a yard from the horses—the prisoner was riding the foremost of the five horses—this happened opposite a coach stand—we were all on the left of the coach stand—the prisoner came along-side with the string of horses, between the deceased and the footway—thinking there was not room between the waggon and the coaches or cabs, the prisoner, unfortunately for the deceased, came on the near side—there was not room between the waggon, and the hackney coach-stand, for the string of horses to go—I should consider there was about fourteen feet between the coach stand and the waggon—there was fifteen feet on the other side, for I measured it—there was as much room on one side of the waggon as the other, within a foot—the curb was fifteen feet from the near side of the waggon, where the deceased was killed, and fourteen from the other—I heard no words take place—I was about five yards behind the waggon—the prisoner came quietly along, riding on the horse—they were walking at a slow rate—owing to the waggoner holding his whip up, after the horses had escaped the waggon, the horse made a quicker step, touched the deceased, and he fell down—I believe it was a pure accident—the deceased had a whip over his right shoulder—the five horses coming by the wheel of the waggon, seeing the whip in his hand, plunged against the deceased, knocked him down, and he died, as the waggon wheels went over him—he did not die till the wheels went over him—it was about it quarter before seven o'clock in the evening—it was daylight—the waggon had about four tons of coals in it—the deceased was in my sight at the moment he was thrown down—I saw him fall, and I believe it was purely accidental—I never saw the prisoner before—I was in company with the deceased—he had drawn a load of coals to my house that day, and these were going there.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You mentioned that there was a stand of coaches—was it by Rowland Hill's chapel? A. No—it was on this side the chapel, some considerable distance—it was in the Blackfriars'-road, where the coach-stand is—I did not measure the distance between the coach-stand and the waggon, but I should reckon it about fourteen feet—it might have been less—the coaches stood as they usually do—there were carts, cabs, and coaches coming along—I do not think the prisoner could have got through, between the waggon and hackney coach-stand—I should consider he was more likely to have caused an accident if he had gone on the other side—I saw the deceased struck by the furthermost horse from the prisoner, the fifth horse—the waggoner had a regular car-man's whip across his shoulder—the thong might have hung over the left shoulder, and hit a horse or any thing as it passed—seeing the whip, the horse shyed at it, and plunged against the man, and he went down—I do
not believe the prisoner could help the accident—he was going at a slow walk, and was perfectly sober—I attribute no blame to him at all.
COURT. Q. You say there was not room for him to take the five horses, between the off side of the waggon and the coaches? A. I do not think there was—the horses were tied at the shoulder and tail, in the way horse-dealers convey their horses—they came shoulder to shoulder—one horse would be a foot or two feet back—the head of the second horse would be about the first horse's shoulder—they were tied at the shoulder and tail both—I am a coal-dealer—the prisoner's horses were all tied as I have described.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was it poasible to make one horse go behind the other, from the manner they were tied; or must they keep almost abreast, though not entirely so? A. They must keep almost abreast.
JOHN HOWES . I am a cabinet-maker. I was standing at the end of Charles-street, Blackfriars-road—I observed the waggon coming towards me, with four horses, one behind the other—the waggoner was on the right side of his horses; on the near side to the pavement—I am sure he was on the proper side; his left side was towards the pavement, and his right to the horses—he was on the left hand side of the horses—he appeared to be nearest to the second horse from the shaft—the horses were walking—I saw a string of horses come up—I consider there was from fourteen to fifteen feet between the waggon and the pavement—the horses came inside the man, between the footway and the waggon—the five horses were not exactly abreast—I saw the horses passing by—a grey horse, as they passed by, touched the deceased, which caused him to fall—I am not aware which of the five horses it was—I immediately ran and stopped the waggon horses, but not in sufficient time, as both wheels went over the man—he was quite dead immediately—I rather think the horse that threw him down shyed, being the near horse to the deceased.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner did not at all attempt to get away? A. No; I called out to him, and he stopped directly—he was quite a stranger to me—I have heard Franklin's evidence, and I agree with him—car-man might have gone nearer to his own horses, and let the other horses pass by—he was some distance from his horses—if he had been close, the horses would have passed with safety.
COURT. Q. Did you see the deceased turn round and move his hand towards the prisoner to keep him off? A. I saw him move his hand; what it was for, I cannot say; but when the prisoner was coming inside the waggon, his hand rose up, but with what intention I do not know—he was looking towards his own horses, not at the prisoner or his horses—I heard a voice, but I could not tell who it was from—it struck me as very possible that the prisoner might have passed dear, if the deceased had gone closer to his own horses—the whip might be in his hand when he held it up.
WILLIAM LOCKE . I observed the five horses passing the waggon—I was standing about forty yards before the waggon came to me, and just before the man came up with his string of horses, and saw the deceased put his hand up, but what he said or meant, I do not know—the horses were five or six yards from the waggoner when he held up his hand; but, I was before the waggon, and cannot be certain—it was before any of the horses had passed him—he was turning round to the string of horses, but from his appearance I think he was intoxicated, for he was tumbling about—I do not think he was capable of minding his horses—his fact was turned to
the string of horses when he lifted up his hand—what he said I do not know—I did not hear—there was some conversation between them—I do not know what it was—it appeared to me that the deceased was intoxicated—I saw him coming before the horses reached him—he was some times close to his horses, and at other times rolled away from them, just as if he was going to roll on the ground—it appeared to me that he was quite incapable of managing his team—the horses were coming along at a walking pace, and just before the hind horse came up to the carman, he plunged up, and either that or the other horses struck the carman, and he fell down—what he shyed at I cannot say—he was knocked down about three yards before the waggon wheel came up to him—I think he might have had quite sufficient time to get out of the way before the waggon wheels got to him, if he had been sober—the misfortune was owing to the waggoner being drunk—I think he was drunk—the Coroner never asked me about his being drunk—I did not state it.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you an entire stranger to the prisoner? A. I never saw him, to the best of my knowledge, before—the deceased ought to have been much nearer to the curb with his waggon than he was.
HENRY FRANKLIN re-examined. The deceased was conveying coals for me, and was accompanying his waggon from the wharf to my house—he had been drinking, and had been out with coals to other places before—I think he was not sober enough to drive a waggon safely—I did not consider him perfectly safe—but there was another man with him, and instead of his driving, he was on the waggon, and the deceased was driving—the regular carman was on the waggon—I do not say he was so drunk that he could not drive, but he was in liquor—he had been drinking a good deal—I perceived that from his walking about.
NOT GUILTY .
First Jury, before Mr. Recorder.

192. JAMES CLARKSON was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, at Wands worth, 1 goose, value 5 s., the goods of Thomas Flavell.
WILLIAM HALL . I am a labourer, and live on Wandsworth-common. On Saturday, the 25th of October, I saw the prisoner in Mr. Rough's hedge, about half-past one o'clock, driving a goose into the ditch—the prisoner followed it into the ditch, and was there about five minutes—he then rose up before me with the goose under his arm, tied in a handkerchief—it was then dead—I followed him—he threw it down—I took it up and gave it to Flavell, who took the prisoner shortly after.
THOMAS FLAVELL . I am a labourer, and live on Wandsworth-common. Hall produced a goose to me, dead—it was worth 5 s.—I had turned it out that morning, about seven o'clock—it was my property—I took the prisoner into custody myself—he acknowledged it all to me.
THOMAS BICKNELL . I am inspector of the V division of the police, stationed at Wandsworth. On Saturday, the 25th of October, the prisoner was brought to me, in custody, by Flavell, who produced a goose—it was still warm—I have the claws.
THOMAS FLAVELL re-examined. These are the claws of my goose.
Prisoner's Defence. The goose was dead when I found it in the hedge.
GUILTY. Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy .— Confined Three Months.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

193. CORNELIUS DONOUGHUE was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of November, 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l., of George Alderson.
JOSEPH HARRISON . I am in the service of Mr. George Alderson, No. 13,
Wellington-street, Borough. On the 22nd of November, about nine o'clock at night, the prisoner came in and took the trowsers—he went out—I pursued him, and he was taken by the policeman—these are the trowsers.
JAMES ANDREWS . I am a police-constable. I was watching the prisoner—I saw him go into the shop, and come out with the trowsers—he threw them down at the end of Thomas-street, and I took him.

194. SAMUEL REED was indicted for receiving, on the 10th of September, 46lbs. weight of hay, value 2 s.; the property of Stephen Marfleet, well knowing it to have been stolen.
JAMES LOXTON ( police-constable P 236.) On the 18th of September, I was watching, at a quarter before eight o'clock, at the Greyhound public-house, Clapham-road—Osgood, who has been convicted, drove up in a horse and cart—he got out, put the nose-bag on the horse, and then opened the door at the back of the cart, and this prisoner pulled out a bundle of hay, about 46lbs. weight—he took it into the stable, followed by Osgood and another man, carrying a sack with some corn in it, which had also been taken from the cart—I followed them, and saw the prisoner throw the hay under a stair-case in the stable—I took him into custody—he struggled violently and got from me—he seized a pitch-fork, made a thrust at me, and said, "D—n your eyes, now come on, I will not be taken"—he got away then, but on a subsequent occasion I took him—he refused to go till I told him the charge, and he resisted very much—I called two brother officers, and he was taken.
Prisoner. I was called to give the horse some water, and while I was doing that, another man took the corn—I took the hay, but I did not know it had been stolen—the other officer told him to knock the b—r down. Witness. No; my brother officer said, "Knock him down," which I should have done—but I could not get at him for the fork.
STEPHEN MARFLEET . William Osgood was my convicted for stealing this hay at the last Sessions.
GEORGE TWOAT ( police-constable P 18.) I went into the stable, and saw the prisoner throw the hay off—I was engaged with Osgood and the other prisoner, who have been convicted—I called to Loxton to knock the prisoner down, but not till he had got the fork, and I thought he would run him through.
Prisoner. I said I would go any where. Witness. No; you said you would not be taken, and you took the fork up—there were four of them there, and the prisoner escaped.
JURY. Q. What was the prisoner? A. I believe he was under-ostler—the ostler has been convicted.
EDWARD EDMONDS . I am a clerk to Mr. Lewis, the attorney. I was present at the trial of Skelton, who was ostler and occupier of this stable—it was sworn on the trial, by the policeman, that he directed three persons to take the hay into his stable, as they supposed Osgood would return from town there to feed his horses—I have seen the stable, and the place in which this hay was thrown is not an improper place to put it into—Skelton was acquitted, and it appeared that this prisoner is one who hangs about as a cad, to get a shilling of any passengers who call there.
COURT to LOXTON. Q. You say, upon the cart driving up, the prisoner went to the back of the cart, and pulled out the hay? A. Yes—Skelton was not at the cart, but I believe he was about there—he had not an opportunity
of speaking to the prisoner between the time of the cart driving up, and his taking the hay.
COURT to GEORGE TWOAT. Q. Did you see the cart drive up? A. Yes—I was within fifty yards of it—I kept my attention on it—Skelton did not communicate with the prisoner after the cart drove up, and before the hay was taken; if he had, I must have seen him.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18341124-195]
195. JOHN SPARROW was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of October, 1 ring, value 10 s.; and 1 necklace, value 10 s.; the goods of Thomas Irving; and WILLIAM SHAW and RICHARD SHAW were indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c.
ELIZABETH IRVING . I am wife of Thomas Irving. I live at Lambeth—on the 14th of October, I missed, from the drawers in my bed-room, a ring and a coral necklace—there was an odd bead in the same paper with them—these are the ring and necklace—Sparrow lodged in the house with his parents.
Cross-examined by MR. MAHON. Q. Had you always the same impression that you lost these things from your bed-room? A. Yes; my husband wished me write to Bath respecting them, which I did—I had been at Bath seven weeks previous to my missing these things—I had them with me there, and to satisfy my husband, I wrote there, but it was not to satisfy myself—I had an answer directly, that I had not left them there—I know I put them in the drawer in my bed-room, on the 12th of October—I let the first and second floor of my house—Sparrow's father and mother lodged in the house, and he with them—my bed-room door was locked at night, but I left the key in it—my lodgers were out the day I missed these things—I have no servant—I have a char-woman—she has cleaned my bed-room, but did not at that time.
CHARLES DAVIS . I was standing minding my mother's stall—Sparrow came up and showed me a ring on his finger—I asked him where he got it—he replied, "That is my business"—he showed me one coral bead rather larger than a grey pea—I asked him to give me that—he said, "O no, it is worth 3 d."—Mucklow came up at the time—this ring is like the one he showed me—I believe it is it, but cannot swear to it.
Cross-examined. Q. You know nothing about the necklace? A. No; I have not seen rings like this in jewellers' windows—I cannot swear to it—I did not have it in my hand.
PETER PALMER . I am shopman to a pawnbroker. I produce this coral necklace, which was pawned by William shaw for 3 s. 6 d., on the 23rd of October.
Cross-examined. Q. This is a mere common necklace? A. No, it is a very good one—one bead of coral resembles another—our shop is in New Park-street, South wark—no one came to pawn a necklace that day, but William Shaw—I could not swear to every one who came; but I know him—he had pawned for his mother before.
JOHN WALKER . I am shopman to a pawnbroker. I have a ring pawned for 3 s., but I cannot recollect by whom—this is the duplicate I gave.
EDWARD LANGLEY ( police-constable L 148.) I apprehended Richard Shaw—he said he had the duplicates to take care of for Sparrow, but he did not pawn the things—that his brother Bill pawned them, and he had given the duplicates to the prisoners—I then went and took William Shaw—
when I got to the house, I inquired for him—seme children said he was not at home, but I found him in the privy—I told him he must come with me respecting the necklace—he said he had pawned it, but he did not know it was stolen, and all he had for it was a glass of gin and cloves.
ELIZABETH IRVING re-examined. I had seen Richard Shaw the evening before, when Sparrow's mother met him in Blackfriars-road, and she called me out of a baker's shop—I asked him if be knew any thing about the necklace and ring.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not promise not to prosecute him, and say you would not hurt a hair of his head? A. No, I did not—I said I should like to have my necklace and ring—I did not care so much about the sovereign which I had lost, as I did about them.
COURT. Q. What did he say? A. He said, "I have the duplicates of the necklace and ring, which Sparrow gave me; but I did not know they were stolen"—I said, "Will you get me the duplicates?"—he said, "Yes, I will"—I waited up till eleven o'clock, but he did not come—the next day I went to him, and he gave them to me.
SPARROW— GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Four Months.
WILLIAM SHAW
RICHARD SHAW
NOT GUILTY .
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

196. SARAH COONEY was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of November, 1 pair of shoes, value 3 s.; the goods of Joseph Wilkinson; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOSEPH WILKINSON . I live at Kent-street, in the Borough, and am a shoemaker. I lost these boots from my door on the 18th of November—I had seen them safe about one o'clock—one of them was brought to my shop on the Thursday following, to know if I had any larger.
MARY BRADBURY . I was coming from Covent-garden with a load, and met the prisoner—I asked her to help me home, which she did—she then said she had a pair of shoes to sell, belonging to her husband, and she would let me have the duplicate cheap, as she wanted a cup of tea, for she had not broken her fast that day—I bought the duplicate, and got them out—when my son came home, he tried them, and they were too small—I sent one to the prosecutor's, to know if he had any larger—he stopped it.
ELIZABETH MANNING . The prisoner gave me the boots to pawn lor for her, and I pawned them for 1 s.
JAMES COPEMAN . I am an officer. I produce a certificate of the former conviction of the prisoner—I know she is the person.
Prisoners Defence. I went to the Royal Oak—there were several persons there, and one of them asked me to buy the duplicate of these boots for 4 d., which I did—I got them, and as they would not fit my husband, I sent them to be pawned.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18341124-197]
197. FELIX FARNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of November, 2lbs. weight of copper, value 1 s.; and 1/2 lb. weight of brass, value 6 d.; the goods of the Phoenix Gas Light and Coke Company.
EDWARD MCEVOY . I am in the employ of the Phoenix Gas Light and Coke Company. On the 20th of November I was on the premises, and told the prisoner, who was working there, that I was going to Bankside; instead of which I stayed and watched the premises—in about ten minutes I saw the prisoner come out—I watched him into a marine-store shop, and
collared him as he was in the act of taking money for this copper pipe, which was in the scale, but I had before seen it in his hand—we had such copper on our premises—a scuffle ensued—his frock gave way up to the bosom—he slipped it over his head, and ran off.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. What is the title of the Company? A. The Phoenix Gas Light and Coke Company—the prisoner was a labourer—we had lost copper of this description—I saw this in the prisoner's hand before he got into the shop—any one might have seen it who was watching him—it was about eleven o'clock in the day—I do not know whether this is called refuse brass and copper—no one had any right to take it away—the men never take any—these are old gas fittings—it ought to remain on the premises—it lays in a heap—it was in a copper kettle, and this was taken from it—I cannot swear I had seen these pieces on the heap—I know the heap was diminished—the prisoner had been about three months there—no one but Mr. Monro had power to give persons leave to take this copper and brass—he is here.
(Mr. Parnell, a painter and glazier, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy .— Confined One Month.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18341124-198]
198. THOMAS FREEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of October, 13 sovereigns, and 1 £10 Bank Note; the monies and property of Stephen Sellis.
STEPHEN SELLIS . I five in Arlington-street, Lambeth, and am a millwright, in the employ of Mr. Maltby. The prisoner had been in his employ—I had a tool-chest in the top shop, and a small box in it, which contained fourteen or fifteen sovereigns, and a ten-pound note—it was safe a little before the end of September—I did not see it during the month of October—I missed it about three weeks ago—this is the box the money was in.
HANNAH BRYAN . I live in May-pole-alley, and work for Mr. Maltby. The prisoner had worked there, but left four or five months ago—I saw him five or six weeks ago last Wednesday fortnight he came to the cooperage, where the working women have their breakfast and dinner—he came to look for an apron, and went up stairs to the room the prosecutor's box was in—I followed him up stairs, and asked what he wanted—he said an apron—he was then standing by the prosecutor's box—he opened it in my presence, and overhauled the tools that were in the box—he said to me, "Go down stairs, and see if Mr. so and so (the foreman) is in the factory"—I went down—he came down shortly after, and went away—he came again that week, or the week following, at the same time, between one and two o'clock—he went up, but I did not go up then—he did not stay long.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long is it since he went up? A. I do not know—it is five or six weeks since last Wednesday fortnight—I do not know what month it was—I did not know the money was in the box till the man reported it.
Q. How long is it since you first heard the prosecutor had lost his money? A. I do not know when he lost his money—I did not hear of it before the man reported it.
Q. Did you ever hear that the prosecutor had lost his money? A. Why, certainly I heard he had lost his money—it is about six weeks ago, to the best of my knowledge—I did not see the prisoner in the factory before he came that time, since the time he left the factory.
Q. How long was it before you heard the prosecutor had lost his money
that you saw this boy in the factory; or was it after that? A. I did not see him in the factory till he came there after he left the factory—that was before I heard the prosecutor had lost his money.
Q. Was it a day, an hour, or a week? A. I do not know—I had no hand in taking this money—it was about a fortnight after it was gone that I heard of it, to the best of my knowledge—I kept no account of the month—that man's business don't concern me.
Q. How long was it before you heard the money was lost that you saw the prisoner in the tool-room? A. About a fortnight, to the best of my knowledge—he opened the chest in my presence—I do not know whether it had any lock—he was at it before I went up stairs—I do not know whether he had any key—he lifted up the box lid—it was to be opened by any one that came into the room—I might open it, and so might others as well as me—Bridget Riley, one of the women, told me to go up—I have known her getting on for two years— Biddy did not go up with me—there were two more women there, Mary Haggerty and Mary Donovan—they are not here—the women persuaded me to go up to see what the boy wanted—he said he wanted an apron—he went to the box and overhauled the tools, and then turned round and said, "Go down and see if Mr. so and so is in the factory;" he meant Mr. Owen, the foreman.
Q. It was a fortnight afterwards that you heard the money was lost; do not you know that the box was open for that fortnight for any one to go to? A. I suppose it was—I was not up stairs for the whole fortnight—I know there were workmen in the room, but I was not there during the fortnight—I did not know old Mr. Pratt, or young Mr. Pratt, before the money was found with them.
Q. When did you become acquainted with the Pratts? A. I do not know—I did not keep any date—I do not exactly know—I did not know them till after the prosecutor lost his money—about five or six weeks from last Wednesday fortnight—when this money was found, I was brought to Court about it; I then picked up a little acquaintance with old Pratt.
Q. Did you say you were very sorry you were brought forward in this business, and that you knew no more about it than any body else? A. I know what I saw done, and told it—I said I knew no more about it than seeing the boy go up stairs—I will not swear I did not say it.
WILLIAM HENRY PRATT . I live with my father in Bond-street, Lambeth. I know the prisoner by sight—on the 7th of October I saw him digging a hole in the ground with his heel—I went down stairs, washed myself, got a light, and opened the two doors—it was a quarter past seven o'clock in the morning—I afterwards went and dug this box up at the spot—I went to my work and opened the box—as I was going down the Commercial-road, I saw there were some sovereigns and three half-crowns in it—I then went to my work, and mentioned it to a young man, who advised me to wrap the sovereigns in paper, and give them to my father—when the prisoner had made the hole, he dropped this box from his pocket, and took a handfull of chalk and threw it over it.
Cross-examined. Q. Now let me tee if I rightly understand; you saw this little boy digging with his feet—he dropped the box into the hole, covered it up, threw some white chalk, to know it again, and you went and dug it up again? A. Yes; and I took the money to my work, I folded it in some brown paper, and gave it to my mother—that was all the money I found in the box; and my mother gave it to my father—I had none of it afterwards—I know Henry Tomsit—I have conversed with
him about this money—I did not take 2 l. of it to buy a watch—I never said so.
Q. Did you ever tell Tomsit that you had taken 2 l. to buy a watch, that you had been obliged to make the money up again, and that you had been obliged to pawn the watch and box? A. No—I never told Abbott that Marchant I had buried a shilling in a hole, and, in taking it out, had found the box—I told him about finding the box, but I did not say a word about seeing any body bury it, because I did not like to let the boy get into any trouble before I let my father know—I never told William Coudroy I came to find it—I did not tell him I had buried 3 d. there the night before, and, in going to look for that, I found the box—I found fifteen sovereigns and 8 s. 6 d. in the box—I had a chat with Marchant and Tomsit about this business before I went to the police-office, but I swear I did not state what you have mentioned about taking the 2 l. to buy the watch, and then pawning it.
Witnesses for the Defence.
HENRY TOMSITT . I work for Mr. Hincklow, a fire-work maker, in Blackfriars'-road. This witness, William Henry Pratt, has talked to me about the money which he found—he said he took two sovereigns out to buy a watch, and he had been obliged to pawn it and a desk to make the money up again; and he said he saw young Freeman bury it in the corner.
ABBOTT MARCHANT . I work at Mr. Clowes's printing-office. I know this boy Pratt—he told me he put a shilling into the hole, and in looking for the shilling he found the box—he did not say one word about seeing any body burying it.
WILLIAM COUDROY . I am a cold-presser at Mr. Clowes's printing-office. I know Pratt—he told me he put 3 d. into the hole the night before, and in looking for it he found the box—upon my questioning him how he came to put the 3 d. there, he said he was afraid his mother would hear it rattle.
NOT GUILTY .
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

199. MATTHEW ATKINSON was indicted for two assaults with intent to commit unnatural crimes.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18341124-200]
200. JOSEPH RYLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of November, 16 sovereigns, and 1 £5 Bank-note, the property and monies of Charles Ireland.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES IRELAND . I keep a beer-shop at Vauxhall. On the 21st of October, about eight o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to my house—there were persons there—a party at cards was made up—we played at Whist—the prisoner was one—I was one, and there were two other persons played—the cards were dealt, and they wished me to go and fetch the pot of ale for which we were playing—I do not know who asked me to go, but I left my cards on the table, and when I came back the prisoner proposed a bet—he said he had a very excellent hand, and he thought he should win the odd trick—he said, "I will bet I do it"—he looked at me—I said I should not bet—he said he would bet 10 l. or 20 l.—I had a reason why I did not bet—I told him I should not bet—he then proposed it again, still moving his cards about—I said, a third time, I would not bet—he then said I had no money—I told him I had, but I
did not think proper to bet—he again said, "You have no money, I am sure"—I then produced a bag, in which I had game money, which I took out of my left hand trowsers pocket, and put it on the table—there was a £5 note in it, and sixteen or seventeen sovereigns—I am sure there were sixteen—the prisoner instantly took the bag and said, "I shall retain this till we play the game out, and see whether you win the odd trick or not"—I said I should do no such thing—I put down my cards, and refused to play the game—the prisoner got up and went to the door, taking the money with him—he went out—I had not given him any permission to take up my money, to retain it, or to go out with it—he went out at the side door—I thought he had gone out for a necessary purpose; but he was gone, and the rest of the party immediately followed him—I saw him again in the Strand on the 30th of October, and gave him into custody—I had seen him twice before, but had no acquaintance with him—we had some conversation—I was looking for him when I met him in the Strand—he was taken before the Magistrate, and bailed after three examinations—I have never got my money since.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you kept your beer-shop in Vauxhall-road? A. Four years, and upwards—I play at cards at times, but very seldom—many weeks elapse when I do not see a card—we have other amusements when we do not play at cards—we play at quoits and knock-em-down—we play for love, or a pot of beer, or 1 s.—we generally play so—always—never for more.
Q. Did you ever play at quoits for any money? A. Yes, I have done so, for more than a shilling or two—on one occasion we played for 20 l., but not at the beer-shop—I was one who played—I won half of the money—I cannot say how long it is ago—about two months, I think—we played at Clapham—the persons we won the money of, were Magnus, a butler, and Buxton, who, I believe, lives on some property—he is not a friend of mine; not at all—I know but little of him—I have known him three or four months—it may be five, not six—I can well swear it, is not twelve months—I never knew he was in the police—I heard he was one of the Union-hall officers—I never heard he was tried for felony—before I kept this beer-shop, I was a publican for three years—I kept the Red Lion, at Lambeth—this game of whist was played in my tap-room—there were only the four persons present who played at it—when the prisoner came in, there were several persons present; but my time expired at ten o'clock, and others left—I had no appointment with the prisoner to come there that afternoon, to play at quoits; none whatever—I do not know John Cotton—I know this person, ( a witness,) but I understood his name was Colton.
Q. Now, did you never, in presence of Cotton, appoint the prisoner to come to your house that evening, to play quoits? A. Never; but I dare say I can explain it—Ryland came to my house a few days before this, and had several pints of ale with a person at the bar—he then addressed himself to me, and said, was not I a quoits player?—I said I could play, but I was not the best player—he said there was a person at Chelsea, whom he knew, who was anxious to back a man he had against any other, for any sum of money; and if I thought I could play sufficiently, he would get the match for me—I told him I had no objection to see the parties, and if I thought I was able to compete with him, I would play—he told me he would see him that night, and mention it to make an appointment to meet me; and the same afternoon, I received a note from Ryland, (but I have not got it here,) wishing me to meet him at the General Hill, at Chelsea—
I went and saw Ryland—he said he had seen his friend, and he had promised to be there, and he was agreeable to put down 20 l., to make the match—we waited about two hours, and his friend did not come—he expressed his sorrow, and told me he would see him again, and come to my place on the following Sunday or Monday morning, and let me know what he said—he did not come on either of those days, but he came on the Tuesday evening, the 21st, and said his friend had given him 5 l. to make the match with me, and if I did not think that sufficient, I was to meet him at the Spotted Dog, in the Strand, at nine o'clock, and there the money would be made up to any sum I pleased—I wished to know on what particulars the match was to be made, and he pretended to find a paper, (but could not,) stating the terms which his friend had written down—the prisoner is a Chelsea carrier, I believe.
Q. How came you to tell the Jury, just now, that you seldom play, and then only for a shilling or a pot of beer, when you went to find a stranger to get him to play? A. I did so, from what the prisoner said—when I play, it depends on chance whether I win or lose—I won the man's 10 l., and kept it—the money had been deposited—when I played at whist with the prisoner, the money had not been deposited—I never said it was.
Q. Then you never directed your attorney to apply for the restoration of a sum of money deposited in his hands? A. I went to my solicitor, and he asked in what manner I should wish it to proceed—I said, as quietly as possible—I did not wish to make any oration about it—I wanted my money—I did not direct my attorney to apply to him for a sum of money deposited in his hands—I heard his clerk had written—it was on the 21st I lost this money, and I went to the attorney the next day.
Q. How did Mr. Humphreys know where to find him? A. He did not know—he wished me to make inquiries—I understood his cart came to the Spotted Dog in the Strand—I went there and got the address, and then I went to Mr. Humphreys directly.
Q. Then, on the 22nd of October, you knew that he lived at No. 1, Cheyne-row, Chelsea, why did you not go to the cart and take him directly? A. He did not drive the cart, for I watched for several days after Mr. Humphreys showed me his answer—this is it—( read)—"Sir, In answer to your application to me, of the 23rd, I beg to inform you, as a solicitor, I had no money, as stated by you, deposited in my hands by Mr. C. Ireland. I have been advised to answer your letter, and inform you of the same, as whatever inconvenience I may be put to in consequence, all parties concerned shall have due notice previous to their illegal proceeding, as threatened by you;—further, if it is the intention of you to proceed, after this notice, (if by bailable process,) my solicitor will give an undertaking to your officer, if you will inform me his name, or will receive any process for me, which will prevent your sending so far.
J. RYLAND."
Q. When did you apprehend him? A. I do not know—I believe it was on the 30th of October—I cannot say when I spoke to the police about it—I spoke to two or three—I took him on the 30th, a little before he got to the Spotted Dog.
Q. Now did not you before the Magistrate state, in the pretence of Mr. Wontner, that you gave no information to any officer, till after you had taken the man into custody? A. I do not recollect that I did—I do not believe I did—I should expect that if I had been asked the question, I should have said I did.
Q. Did not you state in the presence of Mr. Wontner, and on his cross-examination
of you, that you went for no warrant to have him apprehended because you did not know where to find him? A. I did not.
Q. Did not he ask you how it was, if you did not intend, nor make any bet, that you put your money on the table? A. No—I did not make any bet with this man—I put the money on the table to show I had it.
Q. Do you mean to state that you (under all the circumstances that you have now explained) was induced to put the money merely to satisfy an almost entire stranger that you had got money? A. That was my motive—that was the first game we played—we did not play it out—I did not play twenty games afterwards with him—decidedly not—I did not bet ten to one on the odd trick—I did not lose any money after I had lost the bag.
Q. When you were first before the Magistrate, did you state that the prisoner went out of the house alone? A. He went out frequently—he went out alone—I found Grinham was coming to give evidence—I had him taken by the Magistrate's direction—Mr. Minshull was the Magistrate—I did not state, when Grinham was before the Magistrate, that he and the prisoner went away together.
Q. Will you swear that you did not, at the first examination, state that the prisoner went away alone, and afterwards say that he and Grinham went away together? A. No; I could not—I do not believe I could say it, I don't know.
Q. Did not the Magistrate and his clerk both fern to your deposition, and tell you that you had sworn that the prisoner went away alone, and discharge Grinham instantly? A. They discharged him because none of the money was found on him—there was some remark made by one of them—he was not discharged because I at first swore that the prisoner went away alone, and afterwards that Grinham went him—I have seen Grinham about—he was one who was playing at whist, and Shillitoe was the other—I did not have Grinham taken up to prevent his coming forward—I have known him four or five yean—he was not a very intimate acquaintance, but was frequently calling in—I did not lend Grinham money to play, that I will swear—I did not lend him any money that night, that I know of.
Q. Will you swear you did not lend him 30 s.? A. I did not lend him 30 s., or any money whatever—he had no money of mine at til that night, to my knowledge—I do not think I could lend him any money, and have forgotten it—I swear positively I did not.
Q. Did you ask the prisoner whether he would bet twenty to one upon the odd trick? A. No; I did not ask him alter that if he would bet ten to one—I did not tell him he was no sporting man—it was between ten and eleven o'clock when I lost my money—I did not continue to play till between one and two o'clock—I did not play for stakes varying from 2 s. 6 d. to 1 l.
Q. How was Mr. Humphreys to suppose that this money had been deposited by you in the prisoner's hands, if you had not told him? A. He was not at home—his son was—this letter is not stating what I told him—I did not tell Mr.. Humphreys, Senr., that his son had written the letter according to my direction—I did not know whether it was his son or his clerk—I said the letter was according to my direction, but I never said the money was deposited—it was laid on the table.
Q. Did not Mr. Wontner read this note of Mr. Humphreys, describing
the mode in which the money was deposited, before the Magistrate, to you, and did not you state it had been written in those terms by your direction? A. I believe it was to that effect, that it had been written by my orders. I did not state the terms were by my direction—I did not dictate the letter—I did not tell the Magistrate it had been written in these terms by my direction.
Q. You have stated that the prisoner went out of the room, as you supposed, for a necessary purpose, but before he went, he said he should keep this till the game was played out—I suppose you did not ask him what he was going out for? A. No, but I went out in a minute or two afterwards—I found at the Spotted Dog, that he lived in Cheyne-row—I did not go to his house—I thought I should not be able to find him—I was at a loss how to proceed—I have not seen Buxton here to-day—I did not employ him at all in this transaction.
Q. You have stated that the cards were dealt—that you left yours on the table, and went to get a pot of ale—upon your oath, when you were before the Magistrate did you state that? A. I believe I did—I do not know—if I had been asked the question I should—I believe my cards had been improperly dealt with while I was out—I do not know whether I said a word about that before the Magistrate—I should expect I did, for I stated every particular.
Q. On the first occasion before the Magistrate, did you say a single syllable about your having been playing at any game whatever? A. I consider that I must—I explained how the thing occurred—I will swear I did.
Q. Upon your oath, upon the second examination, did not Mr. Minshull complain of your having omitted to state any thing about the game till you came to the second examination? A. I never heard any complaint.
COURT. Q. It has been in the course of this cross-examination, put to you that this was a bet of yours for 20 l., did you put down on the table more than 20 l.? A. Yes, a £5 note, and sixteen or seventeen sovereign.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Will you swear you put on the table more than 20 l? A. Yes, I will—I did not take any of it up again—I will swear I had as many as sixteen sovereigns in the bag—I had counted it about an hour previously.
THOMAS POCOCK ( police-constable F 98.) The prosecutor gave the prisoner into my custody in the Strand, near the Spotted Dog, as he was with a cart—the prisoner asked me what I wanted him for—I told him a gentleman in the road gave him in charge for felony—he seemed quite surprised" but he went to the station very quietly—I told him it was for robbing a person, named Ireland, of 21 l. from his table in the tap-room—I found on him 4 l. 14 s. 9 d.
Witnesses for the Defence.
MICHAEL GRINHAM . I have been acquainted with the prosecutor three or four years—he is in the habit of playing at games for money, sometimes 5 l., sometimes 10 l., as persons choose to play—I have known him to play frequently, within the last three or four months, and I have played with him myself—he will play for any thing that persons choose—I should say he is an acute man—I was at his beer-shop on the 21st of October—I played with him at whist, from a little after ten, till about two—he played all that time, he was my partner—he lent me 30 s. that evening—I owe it him now—the prisoner and Ireland were continually betting on
the odd trick—the prosecutor never refused to bet—he generally proposed to bet—at one time, there was 10 l. betted on the odd trick—the prisoner won it, and it was paid—it was laid down on the table—they went on playing for two or three hours after, and the prosecutor still kept betting on the odd trick, as before—he lost, but I do not know how much—it is not true that he produced some money, upon the prisoner saying that he had not got any, and that the prisoner snatched it up, and ran away with it—no such thing occurred—the prisoner did not take up any money, and, go away with it—he was not so anxious to bet as the prosecutor was—there is no truth in what the prosecutor has sworn, that he only played part of a game—that he did not play out—he continued to play till one or two in the morning.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What are you? A. A baker by trade—I kept a shop near him, about seven years—I was a publican, but I am now out of business—I had a little property when I left my house, and I am expecting a situation—when I let my house, I had money sufficient to keep me—I let it to another party for 150 l —I let it to a person named Brown—his uncle or father keeps a public-house in the Kent-road—Brown has left the house, and I think he went to Chelmsford—I got about 150 l —I cannot tell exactly—I let it for Messrs. Meux—they can tell—I will not swear I got that.
Q. What did you get? A. 136 l. 10 s. I think—I cannot tell exactly—it is twelve months ago—I might owe some debts—I did owe about 30 l —I owed nothing to Meux—I owed my distiller 15 l —his name is Mayston—he lives close by Lambeth-walk—I cannot tell where, upon my honour—I owed my ale brewer, Mr. Goding, of Knightsbridge, about 14 l —his collecting-clerk called on me once a month—I do not know his name, he lived at Vauxhall, but I do not recollect his number—it is close by the turnpike, just in the Wandsworth-road—I never was at his house—I now live at No. 24, John-street, New-road—I have my receipt at home, no doubt.
Q. What time did the prisoner leave this card party? A. I think about two o'clock—we parted good friends—I do not recollect that we shook hands—we were all friendly—there was no complaint on either side—I did not see the bag in which the prosecutor had his money—he paid in sovereigns, and some silver, when the bet was 10 l., there was a £5 note I saw—the prosecutor lost, altogether, about 16 l —the prisoner's partner was a person named Shillitoe—he is a cigar merchant—I do not know where he lives—I have known him about a year—I lost about 2 l., and paid it—I had about 12 s. of my own, and 30 s. I borrowed, and have not paid it—it has not been convenient to pay it.
Q. But you have money? A. No—I did not say I have it now—I had it—I do not know exactly how I am living—I am in view of a situation—I am in no business—my property is nearly gone—I pay every body—I have not paid the prosecutor his 30 s.—he has not asked me for it—I never saw him till he took me no in Bow-street—he took me for this money—he said I had robbed him, and the Magistrate discharged me—I have not seen Shillitoe since—I do not know where he is.
Q. What day of the week was this? A. On a Tuesday, I think, I am not sure—there had been a great deal of ale drank—we were rather fresh—I might have been rather fresh—I was not tipsy—I went home that night—I did not go home with the prisoner—he lived at Chelsea—I parted with him at Charing-cross—I did not go to the prosecutor's from
that time till I was taken—I had been frequently before, but I had no occasion to go there after—I think it was on the 21st of November—it might have been October—I think it is not so long as September—I am not aware that I said it was the 21st—I cannot tell the month; I did not take notice—I think it was October—I will not swear to the month, nor to the day—I swore to the day in question—I did not know that I was obliged to swear—I think I could swear it was not November.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was Shillitoe an acquaintance of Mr. Ireland's? A. I do not know—I was taken to Marylebone-office, and then to Bow-street—the prosecutor there said that I and the prisoner went away together—Mr. Minshull said, "When you were here before, you sware he went away alone, and now you say they went away together;" and he discharged me—I have staid late at the prosecutor's house before I played there.
COURT. Q. Then on this night, whether October, November, or September, there was no quarrel or dispute, or any going away of the prisoner? A. No—he went away about two o'clock—I had been in the habit of going to the house—the amount of the stake was generally 10 s. on every odd trick—there was a bet of 10 l. between the prisoner and the prosecutor—that was the first bet—then was no other of 10 l.—I did not bet—I stuck to my 10 s —the prisoner is a common carrier; he keeps a cart and horse—it was about half-past ten or eleven o'clock—the bet was made, and they played two or three hours after that; I should think, eight or ten games—the prisoner sometimes betted a sovereign with the prosecutor.
JURY. Q. You had no precise sum playing for? A. No—there was a pot of ale a game—there were ten pots of ale drank, or mostly so—the prisoner went out a little before me—I lost about 2 l., but I betted on the trick—Ireland plays at cards, and all games.
JOSEPH SHACKLE . I am an officer of Bow-street. I was present when Ireland was first examined on this charge, and I was there on the second examination—on that occasion the Magistrate remonstrated with the prosecutor on bringing him (Grinham) forward, and swearing that he and the prisoner went away together; when, on the first examination, he swore he went away alone; and he discharged Grinham.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was the prisoner asked what he had to say to the charge? A. He was; and he said they were playing a game at whist and he won the money of the man.
(Thomas Egleton, a tailor, of Lawrence-street, Chelsea; Edward Colton, a cow-keeper, of Sloane-square; James Edward White, a publican, of London-wall; William Bignal, a coach-master, Tothill-street; and Luke Thomas Flood, Esq., a Magistrate of Middlesex; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 34.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury .
Confined Six Months.
ADJOURNED TILL THE FIFTEENTH OF DECEMBER .
